        "text": "  - generic: Access provided by New Jersey Institute of Technology\n      - menuitem \"Login / Register \":\n        - generic \"Login / Register\":\n          - link \"Login / Register \":\n            - /url: /action/showLogin?uri=%2Fdoi%2F10.1061%2FJWRMD5.WRENG-7321\n            - generic: Login / Register\n    - link \"View PDF\":\n      - /url: https://ascelibrary.org/doi/epdf/10.1061/JWRMD5.WRENG-7321\n      - generic: PDF\n      - text: This paper presents a field-oriented methodology for modeling and controlling pressure regulation, demonstrated on a real water distribution network (WDN) sector. The aim is to provide a practical and reproducible pathway from field measurements to deployable real-time pressure control, addressing the gap between simulator-based numerical studies and full-scale field implementations. The study covers data-driven dynamic modeling, controller design, and pre-deployment performance assessment using real operational data. A low-order linear dynamic model with transport delay is identified directly from field measurements, combining 20 days of pressure and inflow data with step-response tests performed on an existing pressure-reducing valve (PRV). The model is validated against an independent 24-h dataset of real consumption, achieving a maximum deviation of approximately\n      - text: at the remote critical node, which is adequate for control-oriented modeling and controller synthesis. Based on the identified model, a digital reference signal tracking (RST) controller with a Smith predictor is designed using explicit performance specifications for settling time, overshoot, and bandwidth. The controller is evaluated in silico using real consumption data not used during identification, providing an unbiased assessment of closed-loop performance under representative demand variability. Results show that the proposed strategy maintains pressure at the remote critical node within acceptable bounds while effectively rejecting demand-induced disturbances. Although closed-loop field implementation is beyond the scope of this study, the framework is explicitly conceived as a preparatory step for real-time deployment. The main conclusion is that a transparent, data-driven methodology grounded in field measurements can reduce modeling and tuning effort while yielding controller designs compatible with standard programmable logic controller (PLC) and supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA) platforms, enabling utilities to progress systematically toward practical real-time pressure control.\n    - paragraph: This study offers practical guidance for water utilities seeking to improve pressure management while reducing water losses and infrastructure wear. Using real operational data, the proposed approach shows how effective pressure regulation strategies can be derived directly from field measurements and implemented with standard PLC and SCADA systems already used by most utilities. As a result, the methodology is accessible without requiring advanced hydraulic-modeling expertise or specialized simulation tools. Pressure management is particularly important because daily demand variations often produce excessive pressures during low consumption periods (e.g., nighttime). These pressure surges increase the likelihood of pipe bursts, background leakage, and premature deterioration of network assets. The proposed control strategy addresses this issue by stabilizing pressure at a critical point in the network, allowing utilities to maintain pressures within acceptable and regulatory limits despite changing demand. While the controller has not yet been deployed in closed-loop field operation, the methodology is designed as a practical step toward real-time implementation. By reducing modeling effort and simplifying controller tuning, the approach lowers technical barriers for utilities interested in advanced pressure control and provides a realistic foundation for improving network reliability and reducing operational costs.\n      - text: ). In particular, Galuppini et al. (\n      - text: ) proposed an in situ identification procedure that estimates frequency responses from measured input–output data via Blackman–Tukey spectral analysis and then fits parametric transfer-function models (including delay), specifically addressing the presence of colored exogenous disturbances. Complementarily, sum-of-delay gray-box structures have been introduced to represent the plant as a superposition of pure-delay contributions; due to the large number of candidate delays, sparse estimation (e.g., LASSO) can be used to reduce model complexity while preserving high-frequency features such as resonance peaks that are relevant for regulator design (\n    - paragraph: This decomposition into a static nonlinear demand–headloss relation and a dynamic linear time-invariant (LTI) model yields a tractable representation suitable for controller design and deployment on standard industrial hardware, without requiring a hydraulic solver.\n      - text: \"): while their focus is field implementation, our contribution is a systematic workflow that enables practitioners to identify an accurate reduced-order model, design a reference signal tracking (RST) controller with explicit performance criteria, and test it off-line with real data prior to deployment.\"\n        - paragraph: Discrete-time RST controller design with Smith predictor. Based on the identified model, we design a digital RST controller with explicit delay compensation, enabling systematic specification of closed-loop dynamics, disturbance rejection, and tracking performance.\n        - paragraph: Implementation readiness. The workflow is designed for implementation on standard PLC/SCADA systems using existing telemetry and PRV hardware, without specialized devices or hydraulic solvers.\n    - paragraph: The remainder of this paper is organized as follows. The subsequent section details the modeling process for remote-node pressure, including the hydraulic sector description, instrumentation, model identification, disturbance modeling, and validation. The next section presents the controller design and the closed-loop evaluation under real demand data. Finally, the paper summarizes the main conclusions and outlines future work.\n    - paragraph: Designing a remote-node pressure regulation system requires a model that relates remote-node pressure dynamics to PRV actuation and variations in consumer demand. container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t90 text This paper presents a field-oriented methodology for modeling and controlling pressure regulation, demonstrated on a real water distribution network (WDN) sector. The aim is to provide a practical and reproducible pathway from field measurements to deployable real-time pressure control, addressing the gap between simulator-based numerical studies and full-scale field implementations. The study covers data-driven dynamic modeling, controller design, and pre-deployment performance assessment using real operational data. A low-order linear dynamic model with transport delay is identified directly from field measurements, combining 20 days of pressure and inflow data with step-response tests performed on an existing pressure-reducing valve (PRV). The model is validated against an independent 24-h dataset of real consumption, achieving a maximum deviation of approximately \n\t\t\t\t\t\t91 container plus or minus 1 normal m, ID: i001\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t92 container plus or minus 1 normal m\n\t\t\t\t\t\t93 container normal upper H 2 normal upper O, ID: i002\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t94 container normal upper H 2 normal upper O\n\t\t\t\t\t\t95 text  at the remote critical node, which is adequate for control-oriented modeling and controller synthesis. Based on the identified model, a digital reference signal tracking (RST) controller with a Smith predictor is designed using explicit performance specifications for settling time, overshoot, and bandwidth. The controller is evaluated in silico using real consumption data not used during identification, providing an unbiased assessment of closed-loop performance under representative demand variability. Results show that the proposed strategy maintains pressure at the remote critical node within acceptable bounds while effectively rejecting demand-induced disturbances. Although closed-loop field implementation is beyond the scope of this study, the framework is explicitly conceived as a preparatory step for real-time deployment. The main conclusion is that a transparent, data-driven methodology grounded in field measurements can reduce modeling and tuning effort while yielding controller designs compatible with standard programmable logic controller (PLC) and supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA) platforms, enabling utilities to progress systematically toward practical real-time pressure control.\n\t\t\t\t96 container executive-summary\n\t\t\t\t\t97 heading Practical Applications, Value: 2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t98 text Practical Applications\n\t\t\t\t\t99 text This study offers practical guidance for water utilities seeking to improve pressure management while reducing water losses and infrastructure wear. Using real operational data, the proposed approach shows how effective pressure regulation strategies can be derived directly from field measurements and implemented with standard PLC and SCADA systems already used by most utilities. As a result, the methodology is accessible without requiring advanced hydraulic-modeling expertise or specialized simulation tools. Pressure management is particularly important because daily demand variations often produce excessive pressures during low consumption periods (e.g., nighttime). These pressure surges increase the likelihood of pipe bursts, background leakage, and premature deterioration of network assets. The proposed control strategy addresses this issue by stabilizing pressure at a critical point in the network, allowing utilities to maintain pressures within acceptable and regulatory limits despite changing demand. While the controller has not yet been deployed in closed-loop field operation, the methodology is designed as a practical step toward real-time implementation. By reducing modeling effort and simplifying controller tuning, the approach lowers technical barriers for utilities interested in advanced pressure control and provides a realistic foundation for improving network reliability and reducing operational costs.\n\t\t\t\t100 container bodymatter\n\t\t\t\t\t101 container sec-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t102 heading Introduction, Value: 2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t103 text Introduction\n\t\t\t\t\t\t104 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t105 text Water utilities must deliver potable water at adequate flow and pressure while minimizing water losses, operational costs, and asset deterioration. Modern water distribution networks (WDNs) exhibit pronounced intraday and seasonal demand variability, leading to large fluctuations in service pressure (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t106 link Bello et al. 2019, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c3-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t107 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t108 link Fan et al. 2017, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c16-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t109 text ). When pressures exceed acceptable limits—particularly during low-demand periods—background leakage increases, pipe failure risk rises, and infrastructure lifetime is reduced (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t110 link Van Zyl et al. 2008, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c43-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t111 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t112 link Page 2018, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c35-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t113 text ). Stabilizing and reducing pressure is therefore among the most effective strategies to mitigate leakage and bursts, with substantial long-term economic benefits (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t114 link Vicente et al. 2016, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c44-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t115 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t116 link Creaco and Walski 2017, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c12-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t117 text ).\n\t\t\t\t\t\t118 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t119 text In practice, pressure management typically relies on pressure-reducing valves (PRVs) operated under relatively simple control schemes (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t120 link Bello et al. 2019, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c3-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t121 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t122 link Campisano et al. 2010, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c5-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t123 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t124 link Creaco et al. 2019, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c10-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t125 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t126 link Galuppini et al. 2019, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c26-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t127 text ). A common solution is the mechanically piloted PRV with a fixed downstream setpoint (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t128 link Doghri et al. 2022, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c15-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t129 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t130 link Ferrarese and Malavasi 2022, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c17-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t131 text ). More advanced electronic add-on controllers modulate outlet pressure using predefined day–night profiles (time-based control) or as a function of inlet flow (flow-modulated control) to approximately maintain acceptable pressures at remote locations using surrogate signals (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t132 link Campisano et al. 2016, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c6-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t133 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t134 link Fontana et al. 2018b, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c19-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t135 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t136 link Galuppini et al. 2019, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c26-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t137 text ). Remote real-time control extends these concepts by telemetering pressure at one or more critical nodes and continuously adjusting PRV setpoints; laboratory and field studies report effective leakage reduction and improved pressure stability (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t138 link Campisano et al. 2016, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c6-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t139 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t140 link Fontana et al. 2018a, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c18-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t141 text , \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t142 link b, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c19-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t143 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t144 link Galuppini et al. 2019, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c26-3\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t145 text ). Comparative studies further confirm that remote-node–based regulation typically outperforms fixed, time-based, and flow-modulated strategies in pressure stability and leakage reduction, albeit with increased sensing and communication requirements (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t146 link Koşucu and Demirel 2024, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c32-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t147 text ).\n\t\t\t\t\t\t148 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t149 text Motivated by the limitations of fixed-pressure and open-loop modulation, research has increasingly focused on real-time control (RTC) strategies that continuously adjust PRV outlet pressure based on measured network conditions. Early numerical and laboratory investigations demonstrated the potential of RTC to reduce average pressure and leakage (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t150 link Campisano et al. 2010, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c5-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t151 text , \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t152 link 2012, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c7-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t153 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t154 link Creaco and Franchini 2013, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c11-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t155 text ). Subsequent studies introduced calibration methods for proportional or flow-dependent controllers (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t156 link Campisano et al. 2012, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c7-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t157 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t158 link Page et al. 2017, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c36-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t159 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t160 link Page and Creaco 2019, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c34-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t161 text ) and explored RTC architectures for practical deployment (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t162 link Campisano et al. 2016, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c6-3\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t163 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t164 link Fontana et al. 2018a, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c18-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t165 text , \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t166 link b, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c19-3\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t167 text ). Reviews identify RTC as a promising direction but emphasize the need for methodologies that integrate model development, controller design, and practical implementation guidance (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t168 link Vicente et al. 2016, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c44-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t169 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t170 link Creaco et al. 2019, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c10-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t171 text ). Recent work also highlights the importance of pressure regulation strategies that explicitly target leakage reduction under low-demand conditions, such as nighttime pressure management (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t172 link Denardi et al. 2024, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c14-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t173 text ).\n\t\t\t\t\t\t174 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t175 text The dynamic behavior of PRVs has also been studied extensively. Prescott and Ulanicki (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t176 link 2003, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c37-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t177 text ) showed that PRVs exhibit strongly nonlinear dynamics, and that linear simplifications can miss important behaviors, particularly near low valve openings. More recent studies characterized PRV responses under varying operating conditions and control modes (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t178 link Doghri et al. 2022, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c15-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t179 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t180 link Ferrarese and Malavasi 2022, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c17-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t181 text ), while detailed analyses showed that electronically actuated PRVs may become unstable when valve–network gain increases sharply at small openings or when sampling is not properly selected (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t182 link Janus and Ulanicki 2018, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c30-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t183 text , \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t184 link 2021, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c31-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t185 text ). These findings align with work emphasizing the need for controller-oriented dynamic models that explicitly account for delay and gain variation when PRVs act as feedback-control actuators (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t186 link Ulanicki and Beaujean 2021, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c41-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t187 text ).\n\t\t\t\t\t\t188 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t189 text At the same time, the availability of telemetry, remote actuators, and wireless communications has enabled a range of industrial pressure-control solutions. In many utilities, PRVs still operate in fixed-pressure mode (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t190 link Doghri et al. 2022, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c15-3\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t191 text ). Commercial retrofit controllers offer scheduled, flow-based, adaptive, and closed-loop modes with telemetry and remote configuration (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t192 link Technolog Limited 2015, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c39-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t193 text , \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t194 link 2017, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c40-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t195 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t196 link i2O Water Ltd. 2012, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c28-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t197 text , \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t198 link 2013, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c29-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t199 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t200 link BERMAD Water Technologies 2023, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c4-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t201 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t202 link Cla-Val Company 2021, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c9-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t203 text ), demonstrating the maturity of RTC technologies in operational settings.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t204 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t205 text Despite their effectiveness, commercial controllers are often configured through engineering judgment and heuristic tuning. Proprietary algorithms typically do not expose PRV–network dynamics, transport delays, or remote pressure interactions, limiting the ability to assess stability margins or predict closed-loop behavior under varying conditions (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t206 link Creaco et al. 2019, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c10-3\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t207 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t208 link Janus and Ulanicki 2018, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c30-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t209 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t210 link Galuppini et al. 2020a, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c20-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t211 text ). This motivates transparent, reproducible, model-based methodologies that support systematic tuning, predictable performance, and implementation on standard programmable logic controller (PLC)/supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA) platforms.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t212 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t213 text More advanced schemes, including model predictive control, optimization-based approaches, and learning-based methods, have recently been proposed for multinode pressure regulation (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t214 link Galuppini et al. 2023a, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c24-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t215 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t216 link Castelletti et al. 2023, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c8-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t217 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t218 link Denardi et al. 2024, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c14-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t219 text ). Reinforcement-learning–based strategies combined with hydraulic simulation further illustrate the potential of data-driven control under uncertainty, albeit at the expense of increased modeling complexity, computational effort, and limited transparency (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t220 link Belfadil et al. 2024, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c2-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t221 text ). Similarly, recent work has explored integrated pressure management, energy recovery, and economic optimization frameworks (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t222 link Hammond et al. 2024, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c27-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t223 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t224 link Maio et al. 2024, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c33-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t225 text ). While promising, these approaches are often difficult to deploy within the constraints of typical district metered areas (DMAs), where pressure regulation is performed by a single PRV, and implementation simplicity, robustness, and interpretability remain critical.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t226 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t227 text Over the last few years, controller-oriented modeling and digital design/tuning workflows that explicitly leverage WDN measurements have been developed. In particular, Galuppini et al. (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t228 link 2022, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c23-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t229 text ) proposed an in situ identification procedure that estimates frequency responses from measured input–output data via Blackman–Tukey spectral analysis and then fits parametric transfer-function models (including delay), specifically addressing the presence of colored exogenous disturbances. Complementarily, sum-of-delay gray-box structures have been introduced to represent the plant as a superposition of pure-delay contributions; due to the large number of candidate delays, sparse estimation (e.g., LASSO) can be used to reduce model complexity while preserving high-frequency features such as resonance peaks that are relevant for regulator design (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t230 link Galuppini et al. 2021, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c22-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t231 text ). On the controller side, optimization-based tuning frameworks have been proposed to select digital regulator parameters under explicit robustness/performance constraints, including stability margin indicators and sampling/discretization-related limits (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t232 link Galuppini et al. 2020b, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c21-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t233 text ).\n\t\t\t\t\t\t234 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t235 text However, despite these advances, few case studies report a complete and reproducible measurement-to-controller workflow on a real hydraulic sector using routine operational data and an implementation-ready control structure for standard industrial platforms. This work addresses this gap by proposing a complete, data-driven, experimentally grounded methodology for modeling and regulating pressure at a remote critical node in a real hydraulic sector. Although the controller is validated numerically rather than in closed-loop field operation, the methodology relies on measurements from the real network rather than on synthetic models. is identified from real measurements rather than a simulator; (2) the resulting low-order model is explicitly tailored for PLC/SCADA implementation; and (3) validation uses real consumption scenarios rather than artificially generated demand patterns. In this sense, the proposed methodology complements field studies such as Fontana et al. (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t257 link 2018b, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c19-4\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t258 text ): while their focus is field implementation, our contribution is a systematic workflow that enables practitioners to identify an accurate reduced-order model, design a reference signal tracking (RST) controller with explicit performance criteria, and test it off-line with real data prior to deployment.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t259 container sec-1-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t260 heading Contributions and Gap Addressed, Value: 3\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t261 text Contributions and Gap Addressed\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t262 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t263 text This paper bridges advanced RTC concepts and practical deployment by providing a transparent, data-driven, repeatable methodology for modeling and controlling pressure at a remote critical node in a real WDN sector. The main contributions are:\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t264 content list\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t265 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t266 text 1. Data-driven dynamic modeling of a PRV–network sector.  Using field measurements, we identify a low-order linear dynamic model with transport delay linking PRV pilot actuation to remote-node pressure. Static nonlinear demand–headloss behavior is separated from the dynamic response, yielding a controller-oriented representation.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t267 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t268 text 2. Discrete-time RST controller design with Smith predictor.  Based on the identified model, we design a digital RST controller with explicit delay compensation, enabling systematic specification of closed-loop dynamics, disturbance rejection, and tracking performance.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t269 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t270 text 3. Validation with real consumption data.  The controller is tested in silico using an independent 24-h dataset of real consumption patterns, demonstrating accurate pressure regulation and robustness to demand variability.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t271 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t272 text 4. Implementation readiness.  The workflow is designed for implementation on standard PLC/SCADA systems using existing telemetry and PRV hardware, without specialized devices or hydraulic solvers.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t273 text A “Limitations and Future Work” subsection clarifies that the controller is validated only in silico and that field deployment is the natural next step.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t274 text The remainder of this paper is organized as follows. The subsequent section details the modeling process for remote-node pressure, including the hydraulic sector description, instrumentation, model identification, disturbance modeling, and validation. The next section presents the controller design and the closed-loop evaluation under real demand data. Finally, the paper summarizes the main conclusions and outlines future work.\n\t\t\t\t\t275 container sec-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t276 heading Modeling of Remote-Node Pressure, Value: 2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t277 text Modeling of Remote-Node Pressure\n\t\t\t\t\t\t278 text Designing a remote-node pressure regulation system requires a model that relates remote-node pressure dynamics to PRV actuation and variations in consumer demand. This section describes the hydraulic sector, instrumentation, and the identification procedure for the disturbance and dynamic models.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t279 container sec-2-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t280 heading Hydraulic Sector, Value: 3\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t281 text Hydraulic Sector\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t282 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t283 text The study sector is located in Palmira (Valle del Cauca), Colombia. It is predominantly residential, with approximately 7,000 subscribers and an area of about \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t284 container 1 k m squared, ID: i007\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t285 container 1 k m squared\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t286 text . The elevation difference between the lowest and highest points is approximately 14 m. The remote node (the highest point) lies approximately 3 m above the PRV location. All experiments reported here were carried out on the duty PRV with the standby valve closed.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t302 container sec-2-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t303 heading Instrumentation of the Hydraulic Sector, Value: 3\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t304 text Instrumentation of the Hydraulic Sector\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t305 text The duty PRV is equipped with a mechanical spring–diaphragm pressure-reducing pilot (Singer model 160) with a nominal outlet-pressure adjustment range of 20–200 psi. For a node at elevation \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t334 container z, ID: i017\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t335 container z\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t336 text , the hydraulic head is \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t337 container h equals z plus p divided by gamma, ID: i018\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t338 container h equals z plus p divided by gamma\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t339 text , where \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t340 container p, ID: i019\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t341 container p\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t342 text  is gauge pressure and \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t343 container gamma, ID: i020\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t344 container gamma\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t345 text  is the specific weight of water. Because sensor elevations are fixed and flow velocities are low, variations in measured head are equivalent to variations in nodal pressure. Accordingly, we use pressure as shorthand for pressure head. The disturbance model introduced in Eq. (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t346 link Description: 2, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t347 text ) uses the head loss, \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t348 container h Subscript l, ID: i021\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t349 container h Subscript l\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t350 text , between the PRV outlet and the remote node, which includes elevation and frictional losses.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t351 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t352 text Sensor data are transmitted to and stored in a data logger. Controller synthesis requires (1) a dynamic model linking the normalized pilot downstream setpoint, \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t363 container x Subscript p Baseline left parenthesis t right parenthesis, ID: i023\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t364 container x Subscript p Baseline left parenthesis t right parenthesis\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t365 text , to the remote-node pressure head, \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t366 container h Subscript m Baseline left parenthesis t right parenthesis, ID: i024\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t367 container h Subscript m Baseline left parenthesis t right parenthesis\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t368 text ; and (2) a disturbance model describing average head loss between the PRV outlet and the remote node as a function of demand.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t369 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t370 button Open Fig. 2 in Viewer\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t371 container f2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t372 image A system block diagram shows an input x subscript p of t or X subscript p of S passing through a P R V, producing an output h subscript d of t or H subscript d of S, which enters W D N, along with an input q subscript in and h subscript l of (q subscript in) and outputs h subscript m of t or H subscript m of S. The equation G subscript p of s equals H subscript m of S over X subscript p of S is displayed below the diagram.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t373 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t374 text Fig. 2 . System block diagram.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t375 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t376 text WDNs are inherently nonlinear, largely because friction losses scale approximately with the square of flow and because of complex hydraulic interactions. For regulation around a nominal operating point, however, it is standard practice to approximate dominant dynamics with a linear time-invariant (LTI) model (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t377 link Åström and Murray 2021, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c1-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t378 text ). In this work, nonlinearities and slow parameter variations not captured by the LTI model are treated as disturbances. The choice of an RST controller with integral action is deliberate: integral action removes steady-state errors caused by model mismatch and low-frequency disturbances (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t379 link Villanova and Visioli 2012, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c45-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t380 text ), ensuring convergence of remote-node pressure to its reference.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t381 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t382 text The workflow is illustrated in Fig. \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t383 link Description: 3, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t384 text . First, long-term SCADA measurements are used to identify the average behavior of the sector by fitting a static head-loss relationship, \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t385 container h Subscript l Baseline left parenthesis q Subscript i n Baseline right parenthesis, ID: i025\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t386 container h Subscript l Baseline left parenthesis q Subscript i n Baseline right parenthesis\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t387 text , between the PRV outlet and the remote node [Eq. (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t388 link Description: 3, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t389 text )]. This step is conceptually aligned with field-oriented procedures for local and remote node pressure control that extract representative sector behavior from operational data [e.g., (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t390 link Creaco and Walski 2018, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c13-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t391 text )]. Second, PRV step-response experiments are performed to identify a low-order dynamic model with delay between the normalized pilot downstream setpoint, \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t392 container x Subscript p Baseline left parenthesis t right parenthesis, ID: i026\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t393 container x Subscript p Baseline left parenthesis t right parenthesis\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t394 text , and the remote-node pressure head, \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t395 container h Subscript m Baseline left parenthesis t right parenthesis, ID: i027\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t396 container h Subscript m Baseline left parenthesis t right parenthesis\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t397 text .\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t398 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t399 button Open Fig. 3 in Viewer\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t400 container f3\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t401 image A flowchart shows the development of a control-oriented model using Historical and Real-time data. The key difference is that the identification data are obtained directly from field step tests on the real sector, rather than from simulated perturbations. In addition, stochastic demand effects are represented separately through the stationary head-loss disturbance model, so Eq. (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t705 link Description: 4, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t706 text ) captures only the controllable PRV–remote-node dynamics.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t707 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t708 text The gain \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t709 container upper K Subscript m, ID: i091\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t710 container upper K Subscript m\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t711 text  in Eq. (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t712 link Description: 4, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t713 text ) is treated as constant for linear controller design. In practice, PRV gain may vary with operating point; Janus and Ulanicki (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t714 link 2018, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c30-3\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t715 text ) showed that electronically controlled PRVs can exhibit sharp gain increases near closure, leading to oscillations or instability. Here, experiments covered approximately 17–45 m \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t716 container normal upper H 2 normal upper O, ID: i092\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t717 container normal upper H 2 normal upper O\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t718 text  downstream setpoints (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t719 container x Subscript p Baseline element of left bracket 17 percent sign comma 45 percent sign right bracket, ID: i093\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t720 container x Subscript p Baseline element of left bracket 17 percent sign comma 45 percent sign right bracket\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t721 text ), well away from near-closure conditions. Over this range, step responses show consistent time constants and settling times, supporting the use of a single LTI model. For other valves or wider operating ranges, gain variation should be assessed and gain scheduling or nonlinear compensation may be required. Recent RTC developments explicitly address gain variability by adjusting controller gain online while providing closed-loop stability guarantees, e.g., gain-scheduled internal model control (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t722 link Galuppini et al. 2023b, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c25-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t723 text ). Related evidence on low-flow/low-opening oscillatory behavior further motivates gain-variation awareness in PRV control (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t724 link Ulanicki and Skworcow 2014, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c42-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t725 text ).\n\t\t\t\t\t\t726 container sec-2-6\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t727 heading Remote Node Pressure Model Validation, Value: 3\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t728 text Remote Node Pressure Model Validation\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t729 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t730 text Fig. \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t731 link Description: 8, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t732 text  summarizes the combined stationary disturbance model, valve behavior, and remote-node dynamics.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t733 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t734 button Open Fig. 8 in Viewer\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t735 container f8\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t736 image A block diagram with two inputs, q subscript in and x subscript p, undergoing different transformations. The Smith predictor compensates for process dead time, while the RST controller provides disturbance rejection and reference tracking. Similar classical feedback designs have been applied to PRV control, including integral controllers with Smith predictors (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t796 link Fontana et al. 2018b, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c19-5\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t797 text ) and linear optimal regulators (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t798 link Galuppini et al. 2019, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c26-5\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t799 text ). The contribution here is the complete field-driven workflow from data collection and model identification to explicit computation of implementable RST polynomials.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t800 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t801 text The controller structure is shown in Fig. \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t802 link Description: 11, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t803 text . Closed-loop responses for identified and stress-test gains.\n\t\t\t\t\t1351 container sec-4\n\t\t\t\t\t\t1352 heading Conclusions, Value: 2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1353 text Conclusions\n\t\t\t\t\t\t1354 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1355 text This paper presented a complete data-driven methodology for pressure regulation in WDNs, from field measurements on a real sector to the design of a digital RST controller with Smith predictor. A low-order LTI model with delay for the hydraulic path between a PRV and a remote critical node was identified from operational data and step tests and validated against an independent 24-h consumption profile, with errors of about \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1356 container plus or minus 1 normal m, ID: i207\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1357 container plus or minus 1 normal m\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1358 container normal upper H 2 normal upper O, ID: i208\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1359 container normal upper H 2 normal upper O\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1360 text  at the critical node. The controller was then validated in silico using real consumption data not used during identification, providing an unbiased prediction of closed-loop performance prior to field deployment.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t1361 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1362 text Simulation results under real demand show that the controller rejects demand disturbances and tracks reference changes with zero steady-state error. A formal robustness assessment was additionally performed for the Smith–RST architecture using the full closed-loop path from \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1363 container h Subscript m comma r e f, ID: i209\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1364 container h Subscript m comma r e f\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1365 text  to \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1366 container h Subscript m, ID: i210\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1367 container h Subscript m\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1368 text . Over the tested uncertainty set \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1369 container upper K Subscript m Baseline element of StartSet 0.75 comma 1.00 comma 1.26 EndSet, ID: i211\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1370 container upper K Subscript m Baseline element of StartSet 0.75 comma 1.00 comma 1.26 EndSet\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1371 text , the worst sensitivity peaks are \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1372 container upper M Subscript s Baseline equals 1.846, ID: i212\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1373 container upper M Subscript s Baseline equals 1.846\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1374 text  and \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1375 container upper M Subscript t Baseline equals 1.034, ID: i213\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1376 container upper M Subscript t Baseline equals 1.034\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1377 text  (Fig. \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1378 link Description: 15, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1379 text ). The Bode analysis of \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1380 container upper T Subscript full, ID: i214\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1381 container upper T Subscript full\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1382 text  shows a monotonic reduction of the frequency-domain reserves as gain increases, with \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1383 container upper P upper M Subscript minus 3 d upper B, ID: i215\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1384 container upper P upper M Subscript minus 3 d upper B\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1385 text  decreasing from 82.97° to 1.15° and \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1386 container upper G upper M Subscript upper T Sub Subscript full, ID: i216\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1387 container upper G upper M Subscript upper T Sub Subscript full\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1388 text  from 10.41 to 3.12 dB (Fig. \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1389 link Description: 16, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1390 text ). Time-domain responses within the identified set remain stable and non-oscillatory, with preserved disturbance rejection (Fig. \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1391 link Description: 17, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1392 text ); the additional stress-test case \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1393 container upper K Subscript m Baseline equals 2.00, ID: i217\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1394 container upper K Subscript m Baseline equals 2.00\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1395 text  (outside the identified set) remains stable but exhibits larger transient deviations and control effort, consistent with reduced robustness reserve under stronger gain mismatch.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t1396 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1397 text This robustness result is model-based and limited to the identified uncertainty set; therefore, it should be interpreted as a rigorous verification of the implemented design, not as a full robust-control synthesis (e.g., \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1398 container upper H Subscript infinity, ID: i218\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1399 container upper H Subscript infinity\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1400 text ).\n\t\t\t\t\t\t1401 text Beyond this case study, the main contribution is methodological. By combining a static demand–headloss model and a low-order dynamic model identified from field data with an RST–Smith predictor controller, this paper provides a transparent, replicable framework that can be applied to other DMAs using standard instrumentation and control hardware. In doing so, it helps bridge the gap between simulator-oriented RTC studies, proprietary commercial solutions, and practical model-based procedures that expose PRV–network dynamics and closed-loop performance.\n\t\t\t\t\t1402 container sec-5\n\t\t\t\t\t\t1403 heading Limitations and Future Work, Value: 2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1404 text Limitations and Future Work\n\t\t\t\t\t\t1405 text The present study is limited to in silico validation of the controller using real consumption profiles, without yet deploying the algorithm on the utility’s SCADA/PLC infrastructure. A full field implementation is the next step and will be the subject of future work. This will allow assessing additional performance indicators such as leakage reduction, energy savings, and operational robustness, thereby complementing existing field case studies and providing further evidence on the effectiveness of the proposed methodology. Future work will focus on field implementation of the proposed methodology in operational DMAs, which is a necessary step to fully bridge the gap between conceptual RTC frameworks and routine use in water utilities.\n\t\t\t\t\t1406 container glossary\n\t\t\t\t\t\t1407 heading Notation, Value: 2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1408 text Notation\n\t\t\t\t\t\t1409 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t1410 text The following
